Similar Presidential proclamations, which are still in effect, were
issued on March 22, 1924, in respect of Honduras; on September 15.
1926, in respect of Nicaragua; and on June 29, 1934. in respect of
Cuba.
In accordance with the authority conferred upon him in these
proclamations, th.e Secretary of State will permit the exportation to
China, Cuba, Honduras, and Nicaragua of the arms, ammunition,
and implements of war listed in the President's proclamation of May
1, 1937, only when the Department of State has been informed by
the Chinese Embassy in Washington, the Cuban Embassy in Wash-
ington, the Honduran Legation in Washington, or the Nicaraguan

=== Page 160 === (confidence 1.00)
24
Legation in Washington, as the case may be, that it is the desire of
the government of the country into which the arms, ammunition, or
implements of war are to be imported, that the exportation of the
shipment be authorized.
The bringing about of notification to the Department of State
through the appropriate embassy or legation that the government of
an importing state desires that the exportation of a shipment be
authorized is a matter with regard to which the initiative and respon-
sibility lie with the importing government and the potential shipper.
In compliance with article II of the convention between the United
States and Cuba to suppress smuggling, signed at Habana March 11,
1926, which reads in part as follows:
"The High Contracting Parties agree that clearance of shipments
of merchandise by water, air, or land, from any of the ports of either
country to a port of entry of the other country, shall be denied when
such shipment comprises articles the importation of which is pro-
hibited or restricted in the country to which such shipment is des-
tined, unless in this last case there has been a compliance with the
requisites demanded by the laws of both countries."
and in compliance with the laws of Cuba which restrict the importa-
tion of arms, ammunition, and implements of war of all kinds by
requiring an import permit for each shipment, export licenses for ship-
ments of arms, ammunition, and implements of war to Cuba are re-
quired for the articles enumerated below in addition to the articles
enumerated in the President's proclamation of May 1, 1937:
(1) Arms and small arms using ammunition of caliber .22 or less,
other than those classed as toys.
(2) Spare parts of arms and small arms of all kinds and calibers,
other than those classed as toys, and of guns and machine guns.
(3) Ammunition for the arms and small arms under (1) above.
(4) Sabers, swords, and military machetes with cross-guard hilts.
(5) Explosives as follows: explosive powders of all kinds for all
purposes; nitrocellulose having a nitrogen content of 12 percent or
less; diphenylamine; dynamite of all kinds; nitroglycerine; alkaline
nitrates (ammonium, potassium, and sodium nitrate); nitric acid;
nitrobenzene (essence or oil of mirbane); sulphur; sulphuric acid;
chlorate of potash; and acetones.
(6) Tear gas (C H,COCH₂Cl) and other similar nontoxic gases
and apparatus designed for the storage or the projection of such
gases.
The Secretary of State will permit the exportation to Cuba of the
articles listed above only when the Department of State has been in-
formed by the Cuban Embassy in Washington that it is the desire of
the Cuban Government that the exportation of the shipment be
authorized.
No export licenses will be issued for shipments destined to China,
Cuba, Honduras, or Nicaragua of the appliances and substances

=== Page 161 === (confidence 1.00)
25
listed under category VI in the President's proclamation of May 1,
1937.
In the case of shipments of arms, ammunition, or implements of
war from the United States not ostensibly destined to China, Cuba,
Honduras, or Nicaragua, the Secretary of State may require exporters
to present convincing evidence that they are not destined to any of
those countries and may refuse to issue an export license for the same
until such convincing evidence has been presented to him.
Part VII
SPECIAL PROVISIONS IN REGARD TO EXPORTATION TO SPAIN
A joint resolution of Congress approved January 8, 1937, reads as
follows:
"JOINT RESOLUTION
"To Prohibit the Exportation of Arms, Ammunition, and Imple-
ments of War from the United States to Spain.
"Resolved by the Senate and House of Representatives of the United
States of America in Congress assembled, That during the existence of
the state of civil strife now obtaining in Spain it shall, from and after
the approval of this Resolution be unlawful to export arms, ammuni-
tion, or implements of war from any place in the United States, or
possessions of the United States, to Spain or to any other foreign coun-
try for transshipment to Spain or for use of either of the opposing
forces in Spain. Arms, ammunition, or implements of war, the ex-
portation of which is prohibited by this Resolution, are those enu-
merated in the President's Proclamation No. 2163 of April 10, 1936.
"Licenses heretofore issued under existing law for the exportation
of arms, ammunition, or implements of war to Spain shall, as to all
future exportations thereunder, ipso facto be deemed to be cancelled.
"Whoever in violation of any of the provisions of this Resolution
shall export, or attempt to export, or cause to be exported either di-
rectly or indirectly, arms, ammunition, or implements of war from the
United States or any of its possessions, shall be fined not more than
ten thousand dollars or imprisoned not more than five years, or both.
"When in the judgment of the President the conditions described
in this Resolution have ceased to exist, he shall proclaim such fact, and
the provisions hereof shall thereupon cease to apply.
"Àpproved, January 8, 1937, at 12.30 p.m."
Section 1 of the joint resolution of Congress approved May 1, 1937,
amending the joint resolution approved August 31, 1935, reads as
follows:
"SEction 1. (a) Whenever the President shall find that there ex-
ists a state of war between, or among, two or more foreign states, the
President shall proclaim such fact, and it shall thereafter be unlawful
to export, or attempt to export, or cause to be exported, arms, ammuni-
tion, or implements of war from any place in the United States to any
belligerent state named in such proclamation, or to any neutral state
for transshipment to, or for the use of, any such belligerent state.

=== Page 162 === (confidence 1.00)
26
"(b) The President shall, from time to time, by proclamation, ex-
tend such embargo upon the export of arms, ammunition, or imple-
ments of war to other states as and when they may become involved
in such war.
"(c) Whenever the President shall find that a state of civil strife
exists in a foreign state and that such civil strife is of a magnitude or is
being conducted under such conditions that the export of arms, am-
munition, or implements of war from the United States to such for-
eign state would threaten or endanger the peace of the United States,
the President shall proclaim such fact, and it shall thereafter be un-
lawful to export, or attempt to export, or cause to be exported, arms,
ammunition, or implements of war from any place in the United States
to such foreign state, or to any neutral state for transshipment to, or
for the use of, such foreign state.
"(d) The President shall, from time to time by proclamation,
definitely enumerate the arms, ammunition, and implements of war,
the export of wbich is probibited by this section. The arms, ammuni-
tion, and implements of war so enumerated shall include those enu-
merated in the President's proclamation Numbered 2163, of April 10.
1936, but shall not include raw materials or any other articles or
materials not of the same general character as those enumerated in
the said proclamation, and in the Convention for the Supervision of
the International Trade in Arms and Ammunition and in Implements
of War, signed at Geneva June 17, 1925.
"(e) Whoever, in violation of any of the provisions of this Act,
shall export, or attempt to export, or cause to be exported, arms.
ammunition, or implements of war from the United States shall be
fined not more than $10,000, or imprisoned not more than five years,
or both, and the property, vessel, or vehicle containing the same
shall be subject to the provisions of sections 1 to 8, inclusive, title 6,
chapter 30, of the Act approved June 15, 1917 (40 Stat. 223-225:
U. S. C., 1934 ed., title 22, secs. 238-245).
"(f) In the case of the forfeiture of any arms, ammunition, or
implements of war by reason of a violation of this Act, no public or
private sale shall be required; but such arms, ammunition, or imple-
ments of war shall be delivered to the Secretary of War for such use
or disposal thereof as shall be approved by the President of the
United States.
"(g) Whenever, in the judgment of the President, the conditions
which have caused him to issue any proclamation under the authority
of this section have ceased to exist, he shall revoke the same, and
the provisions of this section shall thereupon cease to apply with
respect to the state or states named in such proclamation, except with
respect to offenses committed, or forfeitures incurred, prior to such
revocation."
Pursuant to the provisions of subsections (c) and (d) of this section,
the President, on May 1, 1937, issued a proclamation as follows:
BY THE PRESIDENT OF THE UNITED STATES OF AMERICA
A PROCLAMATION
WiereAs section 1 of the joint resolution of Congress approved
May 1, 1937, amending the joint resolution entitled "Joint resolution
providing for the prohibition of the export of arms, ammunition, and

=== Page 163 === (confidence 0.99)
27
implements of war to belligerent countries; the prohibition of the
transportation of arms, ammunition, and implements of war by vessels
of the United States for the use of belligerent states; for the registration
and licensing of persons engaged in the business of manufacturing,
exporting, or importing arms, ammunition, or implements of war;
and restricting travel by American citizens on belligerent ships
during war", approved August 31, 1935, as amended February 29,
1936, provides in part as follows:
"Whenever the President shall find that a state of civil strife
exists in a foreign state and that such civil strife is of a magnitude
or is being conducted under such conditions that the export of
arms, ammunition, or implements of war from the United States
to such foreign state would threaten or endanger the peace of
the United States, the President shall proclaim such fact, and
it shall thereafter be unlawful to export, or attempt to export,
or cause to be exported, arms, ammunition, or implements of
war from any place in the United States to such foreign state,
or to any neutral state for transshipment to, or for the use of,
such foreign state."
AND wHEREAs it is further provided by section 1 of the said joint
resolution that
"The President shall, from time to time by proclamation,
definitely enumerate the arms, ammunition, and implements of
war, the export of which is prohibited by this section. The
arms, ammunition, and implements of war so enumerated shall
include those enumerated in the President's proclamation
Numbered 2163, of April 10, 1936, but shall not include raw
materials or any other articles or materials not of the same
general character as those enumerated in the said proclamation.
and in the Convention for the Supervision of the International
Trade in Arms and Ammunition and in Implements of War,
signed at Geneva June 17, 1925."
AND wHEREAs it is further provided by section 1 of the said joint
resolution that
"Whoever, in violation of any of the provisions of this Act,
shall export, or attempt to export, or cause to be exported, arms,
ammunition, or implements of war from the United States shall
be fined not more than $10,000, or imprisoned not more than five
years, or both, and the property, vessel, or vehicle containing the
same shall be subject to the provisions of sections 1 to 8, inclusive,
title 6, chapter 30, of the Act approved June 15, 1917 (40 Stat.
223 225; U. S. C., 1934 ed., title 22, secs. 238–245)."
AND wHEREAs it is further provided by section 1 of the said joint
resolution that
"In the case of the forfeiture of any arms, ammunition, or
implements of war by reason of a violation of this Act, no public
or private sale shall be required; but such arms, ammunition, or
implements of war shall be delivered to the Secretary of War for
such use or disposal thereof as shall be approved by the President
of the United States."

=== Page 164 === (confidence 0.99)

AND wEnEAs it is further provided by section 11 of the said joint
resolution that
"The President may, from time to time, promulgate such rules
and regulations, not inconsistent with law, as may be necessary
and proper to carry out any of the provisions of this Act; and he
may exercise any power or authority conferred on him by this
Act through such officer or officers, or agency or agencies, as he
shall direct."
Now, THeReFoRe, I, FRaNKLIN D. RoosevELt, President of the
United States of America, acting under and by virtue of the authority
conferred on me by the said joint resolution, do hereby proclaim that
a state of civil strife unhappily exists in Spain and that such civil
strife is of a magnitude and is being conducted under such conditions
that the export of arms, ammunition, or im:plements of war from the
United States to Spain would threaten and endanger the peace of the
United States, and I do hereby admonish all citizens of the U'nited
States, or any of its possessions, and all persons residing or being
within the territory or jurisdiction of the United States, or its posses-
sions, to abstain from every violation of the provisions of the joint
resolution above set forth, hereby made effective and applicable to
the export of arms, ammunition, or implements of war from any place
in the United States to Spain or to any other state for transshipment
to, or for the use of, Spain.
And I do hereby declare and proclaim that the articles listed below
shall be considered arms, ammunition, and implements of war for the
purposes of section 1 of the said joint resolution of Congress:
Category I
(1) Rifles and carbines using ammunition in excess of caliber
.22, and barrels for those weapons;
(2) Machine guns, automatic or autoloading rifles, and machine
pistols using ammunition in excess of caliber .22, and barrels for
those weapons;
(3) Guns, howitzers, and mortars of all calibers, their mount-
ings and barrels;
(4) Ammunition in excess of caliber .22 for the arms enumerated
under (1) and (2) above, and cartridge cases or bullets for such
ammunition; filled and unfilled projectiles for the arms enumer-
ated under (3) above;
(5) Grenades, bombs, torpedoes, mines and depth charges,
filled or unfilled, and apparatus for their use or discharge;
(6) Tanks, military armored vehicles, and armored trains.
Category II
Vessels of war of all kinds, including aireraft carriers and sub-
marines, and armor plate for such vessels.
Category III
(1) Aircraft, unassembled, assembled, or dismantled, both
heavier and lighter than air, which are designed, adapted, and
intended for aerial combat by the use of machine guns or of artil-
lery or for the carrying and dropping of bombs, or which are

=== Page 165 === (confidence 0.99)
29
equipped with, or which by reason of design or construction are
prepared for, any of the appliances referred to in paragraph (2)
below;
(2) Aerial gun mounts and frames, bomb racks, torpedo car-
riers, and bomb or torpedo release mechanisms.
Category IV
(1) Revolvers and automatic pistols using ammunition in
excess of caliber .22;
(2) Ammunition in excess of caliber .22 for the arms enumer-
ated under (1) above, and cartridge cases or bullets for such
ammunition.
Category V
(1) Aircraft, unassembled, assembled or dismantled, both
heavier and lighter than air, other than those included in Cate-
gory III:
(2) Propellers or air screws, fuselages, hulls, wings, ail units,
and under-carriage units;
(3) Aircraft engines, unassembled, assembled, or dismantled.
Category VI
1: Livens projectors and flame throwers;
(2) a. Mustard gas (dichlorethyl sulphide);
b. Lewisite ichlorvinyldichlorarsine and dichlordivinyl-
chlorarsine);
c. Methyldichlorarsine;
d. Diphenylchlorarsine;

And I do hereby enjoin upon all officers of the United States,

No export licenses will be issued for shipments destined to Spain of
any of the arms, ammunition, or implements of war enumerated in
the President's proclamation of May 1, 1937.
By virtue of the power delegated to the Secretary of State by this
proclamation of May 1, 1937, to promulgate such rules and regulations

=== Page 167 === (confidence 0.99)
31
not inconsistent with law as may be necessary to carry out any of the
provisions of the joint resolution of Congress approved May 1, 1937,
as made effective by this proclamation, the Secretary of State may
require exporters of any of the arms, ammunition, or implements of
war enumerated in the proclamation to present convincing evidence
that they are not destined to Spain and may refuse to issue an export
license for the same until such convincing evidence has been presented
to him.
Section 6 of the joint resolution of Congress approved May 1, 1937,
reads as follows:
"Sec. 6. (a) Whenever the President shall have issued a proclama-
tion under the authority of section 1 of this Act, it shall thereafter be
unlawful, until such proclamation is revoked, for any American vessel
to carry any arms, ammunition, or implements of war to any belligerent
state, or to any state whcrein civil strife exists, named in such procla-
mation, or to any neutral state for transshipment to, or for the use of,
any such belligerent state or any such state wherein civil strife exists.
"(b) Whoever, in violation of the provisions of this section, shall
take, or attempt to take, or shall authorize, hire, or solicit another to
take, any American vessel carrying such cargo out of port or from
the jurisdiction of the United States shall be fined not more than
$10.000, or imprisoned not more than five years, or both; and, in
addition, such vessel, and her tackle, apparel, furniture, and equip-
ment, and the arms, ammunition, and implements of war on board,
shall be forfeited to the United States."
Section 10 of the same joint resolution reads as follows:
"Sec. 10. Whenever the President shall have issued a proclamation
under the authority of section 1, it shall thereafter be unlawful, until
such proclamation is revoked, for any American vessel engaged in
commerce with any belligerent state, or any state wherein civil strife
exists, named in such proclamation, to be armed or to carry any arma-
ment, arms, ammunition, or implements of war, except small arms and
ammunition therefor which thie President may deem necessary and
shall publicly designate for the preservation of discipline aboard such
vessels."
Under the provisions of this section and by virtue of the power
delegated to the Secretary of State by this proclamation of May 1,
1937, to promulgate such rules and regulations not inconsistent with
law as may be necessary to carry out any of the provisions of the joint
resolution of Congress approved May 1, 1937, the Secretary of State
on May 1, 1937, announced that American vessels engaged in com-
merce with Spain might carry such small arms and ammunition as the
masters of these vessels might deem indispensable for the preservation
of discipline aboard the vessels.

Part VIII
SPECIAL PROVISIONS IN REGARD TO THE EXPORTATION OF TIN-PLATE
SCRAP
The act of Congress approved February 15, 1936, entitled "AN ACT
To provide for the protection and preservation of the domestic
sources of tin" reads as follows:
"Be it enacted by the Senate and House of Representatires of the I nited
States of America in Congress assembled, That, in the interest of
national defense, it is hereby declared to be the policy of Congress
and the purpose and intent of this Act to protect. preserve, and de-
velop domestic sources of tin, to restrain the depletion of domestic
reserves of tin-bearing materials, and to lessen the present costly
and dangerously dependent position of the United States with respect
to resources of tin.
"Sec. 2. There shall not be exported from the United States after
the expiration of sixty days from the enactment of this Act any tin-
plate scrap, except upon license issued by the President of the United
States. The President is authorized to grant licenses upon such con-
ditions and regulations as he may find necessary to assure in the pub-
lic interest fair and equitable consideration to all producers of this
commodity.
"Sec. 3. Any violations of the provisions of this Act shall be a
misdemeanor and shall be punished by a fine of not more than $500
or by imprisonment of not more than one year, or by both such fine
and imprisonment."
On February 16, 1936, the President issued an Executive order as
follows:
EXECUTIVE ORDER
TO PROVIDE FOR THE PROTECTION AND PRESERVATION OF THE
DOMESTIC SOURCES OF TIN
Wereas section 2 of an act of Congress approved February
15, 1936, entitled “AN ACT To provide tor the protection and preser-
vation of the domestic sources of tin," provides:
"There shall not be exported from the United States after the
expiration of sixty days from the enactment of this Act any tin-
plate scrap, except upon license issued by the President of the
United States. The President is authorized to grant licenses
upon such conditions and regulations as he may find necessary to
assure in the public interest fair and equitable consideration to
all producers of this commodity."
Now, Therefore, I, FrankLin DelaNo Roosevelt. President
of the United States, acting under and by virtue of the authority
vested in me by the aforesaid act, do hereby delegate to the Secretary
of State as Chairman of the National Munitions Control Board the
power to grant licenses for the exportation of tin-plate scrap upon
such conditions and under such regulations as he may find necessary
to assure in the public interest fair and equitable consideration to all

=== Page 169 === (confidence 0.95)
33
producers of this commodity, and as he may prescribe by and with
the advice and consent of the Board.
FRAnKLIn D Roosevelt
The White House,
February 16, 1936.
In virtue of the authority vested in him by the Executive order of
February 16, 1936, the Secretary of State by and with the advice
and consent of the National Munitions Control Board prescribed on
December 7, 1936, the following regulations:
"(1) For the purpose of the act the term 'tin-plate scrap'is con-
strued, provisionally, to mean tin-plate clippings, cuttings, stampings,
trimmings, skeleton sheets, and all other miscellaneous pieces of dis-
carded tin plate, which result from (1) the manufacture of tin plate,
or (2) the manufacture of tin-bearing articles from tin plate. As thus
defined, the term 'tin-plate scrap' does not include tin-plate waste
waste, tin-plate cireles, tin-plate strips, tin-plate cobbles, and tin-plate
scroll shear butts, when packed separately and sold as such, and
when not intermingled with tin-plate scrap.
"(2) Blank forms of application for export licenses similar to that
printed below will be furnished by the Secretary of State on request.

Part IX
SPECIAL PROVISIONS IN REGARD TO THE EXPORTATION OF HELIUM
GAS
Section 3 of the act of September 1, 1937 (50 Stat. 885), entitled
"AN ACT Authorizing the conservation, production, exploitation,
and sale of helium gas, a mineral resource pertaining to the national
defense and to the development of commercial aeronautics, author-
izing the acquisition, by purchase or otherwise, by the United States
of properties for the production of helium gas, and for other purposes,"
provides in part as follows:
"(b) That helium not needed for Government use may be produced
and sold upon payment in advance in quantities and under regulations
approved by the President, for medical, scientific, and commercial
use, except that helium may be sold for the inflation of only such
airships as operate in or between the United States and its Territories
and possessions, or between the United States or its territories and
possessions and foreign countries: Provided, That no helium shall be
sold for the inflation of any airship operating between two foreign
countries notwithstanding such airship may also touch at some point
in the United States . . ."
Section 4 of the act provides as follows:
"Sec. 4. No helium ges shall be exported from the United States,
or from its Territories and possessions, until after application has
been made to the Secretary of State and a license authorizing said
exportation has been obtained from him on the joint recommendation
of all of the members of the National Munitions Control Board and
the Secretary of the Interior: Prorided, That under regulations govern-
ing exportation of helium approved by the National Munitions Con-
trol Board and the Secretary of the Interior, export shipments of
quantities of helium that are not of military importance as defined in
said regulations, and which do not exceed a maximum to be specified
therein, may be made under license granted by the Secretary of State
without such specific recommendation. Such regulations shall not
permit accumulations of helium in quantities of military importance
in any foreign country, nor the exportation of helium to countries
named in proclamations of the President issued pursuant to section
1 (a) or (c) of the Neutrality Act of May 1, 1937 (Public Resolution
Numbered 27 of the Seventy-fifth Congress) while such proclamations
are in effect, and shall require exporters to submit a sworn statement
to the Secretary of State showing the quantity, destination, consignee,
and intended use of each proposed exportation.
"Any person violating any of the provisions of this section or of the
regulations made pursuant hereto, shall be guilty of a misdemeanor
and shall be punished by a fine of not more than $5,000 or by imprison-
ment for not more than one year, or by both such fine and imprison-
ment; and the Federal courts of the United States are hereby granted
jurisdiction to try and determine all questions arising under this
section.

=== Page 173 === (confidence 0.95)
37
"The National Munitions Control Board shall include in its Annual
Report to the Congress full information concerning the licenses issued
hereunder, together with such information and data collected by the
Board as may be considered of value in the determination of questions
related to the exportation of helium gas."
Under the authority of the afore-mentioned provisions of law and
in pursuance thereof. the Secretary of State on September 3, 1937,
prescribed and promulgated, with the approval of the National Muni-
tions Control Board and the Secretary of the Interior, the following
regulations governing the export of helium gas:
((1) Wherever the word helium is used in these regulations, it shall
be understood to mean 'contained helium' at standard atmospheric
pressure (14.7 pounds per square inch) and 70° Fahrenheit. The
expression contained helium means the actual quantity of the
element helium (i. e. 100 percent pure helium) present in a mixture
of helium and other gases. Purity determinations shall be made by
usually recognized methods.
"(2) Applications for license to export helium gas shall be sub-
mitted to the Secretary of State on forms similar to that printed below,
copies of which will be furnished by the Secretary of State on request.
Each application must be signed and sworn to in the presence of a
notary public before it is transmitted to the Secretary of State. All
applications must be submitted in duplicate.

"(3) Licenses authorizing export shipments of helium gas for med-
ical, scientific, and commercial use other than aeronautical operations
will be issued by the Secretary of State for quantities not to exceed,
during any one year, to one ultimate consignee or purchaser, 20,000
cubic ieet, and not excceding, during any one year, to the consignees or
purchasers within any one country, 500,000 cubic feet. Applications
forflicense to export helium gas in excess of the quantities stated herein,
for purposes other than acronautical operations, will not be granted
by the Secretary of State without the unanimous recommendation of
the members of the National Munitions Control Board and the Secre-
tary of the Interior.
"(4) All applications for license to export helium gas shall be
accompanied by evidence to show that reasonable safeguards have

=== Page 176 === (confidence 1.00)
40
been adopted to insure that there shall be no unnecessary waste of the
helium gas desired.
"(5) Succeeding applications for license to export helium gas shall
be accompanied by information indicating the manner of disposal of
the helium gas obtained under licenses preceding that applied for.
"(6) Licenses authorizing the exportation of helium gas for use in
commercial or scientific aircraft operations and in quantities con-
sidered by the National Munitions Control Board and the Secretary
of the Interior not to be of military importance will be issued by the
Secretary of State under allotments to exporters which, on the joint
recommendation of the Board and the Secretary of the Interior, will
be granted for a period of not to exceed one year.
"Requests for allotments under paragraph (6) hereof shall be sub-
mitted to the Secretary of State in the form of an affidavit, subscribed
and sworn to before a notary public. Such requests shall identify and
describe the aircraft for use in which the helium gas is intended and
shall include, for the consideration of the National Munit ions Control
Board and the Secretary of the Interior, full information in regard to
the proposed schedule of operations of the aircraft for the period to
which the allotments are to apply. Requests for allotments shall also
be accompanied by evidence showing that reasonable safeguards have
been adopted to insure that there shall be no unnecessary waste of the
helium gas desired and evidence to rebut the presumption that the
quantity stated therein should be considered of military importance.
An estimate of the rate at which the helium gas is to be exported shall
be included in all requests for allotments.
"(8) Succeeding requests for allotments under paragraphs (6) and
(7) hereof shall indicate the manner of di-po-al of the helium gas ol-
tained under the allotment preceding that requested.
"(9) Applications for license to export helium gas under allotments
in accordance with the provisions of paragraphs (6) and (7) hereof
shall be made to the Secretary of State and shall include information
which will adequately identify the aireraft for use in which the helium
gas is intended. Such identification shall be the same as that used in
the request for the allotment under which application is being made.
"Helium gas leaving the United States when used for or intended
for the inflation of an aireraft will not be considered as exported within
the meaning of section 4 of the act when it is the intention of the
owner of the aircraft that it shall remain under American registry and
shall be commanded by a duly certificated United States airman
during the entire period of its sojourn abroad, and when there is no
intention on the part of the owner of the aircraft to dispose of the
helium gas in any foreign country.
"(11) The shipper's export declaration customs form 7525) or
such other document as the Bureau of Customs may require must con-
tain the same information in regard to the quantity and value of the
helium gas as that which appears on the application for license.
"(12) Export licenses and export declarations, or other documents
required by the Bureau of Customs, concerning helium gas must be
filed with the appropriate collector of customs at least 24 hours before
the proposed departure of the shipment from the United States, and,
in the case of a shipment by a seagoing vessel, 24 hours before the
lading of the helium gas."

=== Page 192 === (confidence 0.98)
HOUSE MEMORIAL NO. 1.
BY REPRESENTATIVES THOMAS,
BOGGS, STRONG, and FORDHAM.
WHEREAS, In the interests of humanity and world
peace it becomes necessary that the President of the
United States ind the Congress of the United States
take such action as will deny aid or assistance of any
kind to the country of Japan in the furtherance of the
war against the country of China; and
WHEREAS, Denial of the right to purchase munitions
and materials of war would effectively accomplish such
purpose,
NOW, THEREFORE BE IT RESOLVED BY THE HOUSE OF
REPRESENTATIVES OF THE THIRTY-SECOND GENERAL ASSEMBLY:
That this body memorialize the President of the
United States and the Congress of the United States to
take immediate action to terminate the selling of muni-
tions and war materials by citizens of the United States
to the country of Japan.
BE IT FURTHER RESOLVED, That the Senators and
Representatives of the State of Colorado, in the Congress
of the United States give their support to any measure
that will accomplish the purposes of this resolution, and
that copies of this Memorial be forwarded to the President
of the United States, the President of the Senate and the
Speaker of the House of Representatives of the Congress
of the United States and to the Senators and Representatives
of the State of Colorado in Congress.
liem5 1tigley
SPEAKER OF THE HOUSE.

=== Page 199 === (confidence 0.99)
E PRAOPICAL QUESTIONS PERTAINING TO THE ACTIVITIES OF THE PMERICAN COMTLEE
I. Would it be wise to undertake a re-orientation of our program in order to
make it more inclusive? Would it for example be well for us to press now
for an application of Section 338 of the Tariff Law in order to curtail im-
ports from Japan and thus reduce her purchasing power abroad? Would it be
wise also to work for modification of the present gold purchase policy in
such a way as to reduce Japan's purchasing power? to urge extension of aid
to China? and to press for further consultation with the British government?
Many suggestions along these lines have come to us. Unquestionably each
of these lines of effort has much to b e said for it. But it seems to
me necessary for us to remember that we cannot do everything, that it
woul.d be better to do one thing well than to spread our efforts too
thinly over a wide range of objectives. The degree of success that
we have had thus far is attributable I think in no small measure to
the fact that we have concantrated on one single dramatic issue, at-
tempting through every means at our disposal to get this issue into
the active thinking of as many and as influential people as possible.
To continue and extend that effort with greatly increased intensity
during the weeks immediately ahead is in itself a large undertaking.
But I believe that it can be done and that if that cffort succeeds
the relation of our exports . for war purposes to Japan to these other
questions will become increasingly clear and that we may be making a
more positive contribution toward their solution than if ve attempted
to molve too y problems directly.
This is not to say of course that we must b e blind to these other
questions. On the contrary I believe that ve should make every effort
to encourage serious and thorough study of them in order that our own
central effort may be seen in a truer perspectiv e and that we may be
doing our part toward a larger understanding of the whole situation.
To state it somewhat differently, it would seem that members of our
own group and others who are interested in a broader analysis would
do well during the poriod immediately ahead to contrib ute to the ut-
most of their ability toward a more thorough and comprehensive grasp
of the possibilities and implications of American Far Eastern policy.
At the same t imc it vould seem wise as far as our efforts in the direc-
tion of publicity and of influence upon Congress is concerned, for us
to contimue our primary emphasis upon the question of stopping war sup.
plies and credits to Japan, realizing that success in this will re-
inforce other well conceiv ed lines of policy. This is simply a per-
sonal judgment or hunch and is submitted for your criticism.
II. How detailed and specific should our recommendations be?
Returning from Washington I have a stronger feeling that while we shoul
be prepared when the time for legislation comcs to support a specific
piece of legislation, , our main offort now should be to build up anc
strengthen popular and Congressional sentimont against a contimuation
of our important aid to Japan. Repeatedly persons qualified to express
an opinion urge the need for such a simplified approach. As one per-
son expressed it, if Congress really feels that the people do not like
our contimued aid to Japan and want to see it stopped, then something

=== Page 200 === (confidence 0.99)
SOME PRACTICAL QUESTIONS (cOnT'd)
2.
can be done.
Again this does not mean that we must let up in our effort to find a
formula in simple terms which ve can recommend for support, working
in closest cooperation with such groups as the Committce on Concerted
be carefully worked out are less urgent at this stage than a general
crystallization of public sentiment against further assistance to
Japan for var purposes. This again is submitted for your criticism.
III. What techniques are most useful for making the support of individuals and
groups effoctive?
In general the feeling seems to be that individual lettors, vith the
ring of original sincerity and conviction to government leaders are
more effective than resolution forms. Resolutions of course furnish
opportunity to gain a much vider expression and therefore should not
be neglected. But vhen it is possib le to supplement or reinforce
these by à great many personal letters the effect is greatly increased.
It seems very important that ide und offective expressions of opinion
be sent to both the Administration and individual members of Congress,
especially of course members of thc Foreign Relations and Foreign
Affairs Committees. It seems certain that Congress will be watching
the Administration and the Administration will be vatching Congress
in relation to questions of Foreign Policy hen Congress reconvenes.
Intervicws with individual membera of Congress, cspecially with members
of tho two Committees concerning Foreign Affairs end, very important,
with tho President and the Secretary of State, should be conducted as
soon and as effoctively as possible. It secms particularly important
just now that plans be made for a group to interv iew President Roose-
velt, since Congress vill undoubtedly be looking to him and to the
Stato Deparment for a cloar lead on the Far Eastern situation.
Returning from Washington I also fecl tho necessity more strongly than
ever of a strong corps of interviewers to be in Waslington after Con-
gress reconvenes. To a greater or less cxtent members of both houses will
have receivedlotters, potitions, literature from us and from other or-
ganizations.  . Many of them, not very familiar with the Far Eastern
situation, ill be in a stato of indecision. A group of able men and
women vhose prosence in Washington is ensured by othor organizations and
our Committeo should cxert a real and possibly docisive influenco as
they confer ono by one vith memb ers of the legislature. It is not too
soon to begin planning as definitely as possible for this effort.
Harry B. Price,

=== Page 203 === (confidence 0.99)
THE AMERICAN COLMITTEE FOR NON-PARTICIPATION IN JAPANESE AGGRESSION
The University of Chicago
Norman Wait Harris Memorial Foundation in International Relations
Jnuary 6, 1939.
There is no doubt that the President has power, so far ns municipal law is con-
cerned, to exclude any Japanese articles, which he proclaims, from importation
into the United States under Section 18l, Title 19, United States Code, or Section
338 of the tariff of 1938, provided he concludes that Japan has not made a satis-
factory answer to the allegations of discrimination made in the United States note
of October 6, 1938. He might also, by proclamation, exclude Japan fron the bene-
fits of most-favored nation treatment under the commercial reciprocity act of l934,
amending Section 350 (A) of the tariff act of 1930.
But though he has these powers, if he exercised them, it would appear on the face
that the United States would be vlolatin it treaty with Japan of l9ll. This in
general requires national treatment for the citizens of each country in the terri-
tory of the other under article 1, and most-favored nation treatment in regard to
commerce and navigation, in the territories of each respectively, under articles
4, 8 and 1l. This treaty may be denounced on six months notice, but until it is
so denounced presumably we would not ish to violate it unless there were special
circumstances which permitted us to do that under international lav.
One such circumstance would of course be the prior violation by Japan of the trea-
ty of 19ll. It does not appear, however, th it the allegations made in the note of
October 6 would amount to a violation of this re ty, because under this Japan mere-
ty agrees not to discriminate against American nationals, vessels, or commerce
in ner territories or ports, and it vould not appear that the occupied areas of
manchuria or China can be regarded as Japanese territory. The United States seems
not to have made a direct allegation in the note of October 6 that Japan was vio-
lating the treaty of l9ll.
It is, however, recognized that the withdr wal of tre ty privileges is o type of
reprisal which is permissible in case it is proved that the state acted against
has violated other of its obligations under treaty or international la: with the
state imposing restrictions. Now it is clear that Japan has violated a number of
treaties with the United States, including the Pact of Paris, the Nine-Power
Treaty, and the Hague Convention of 1907 denling with the lavs and customs of war
on land. The United States has recognized officially the Japanese violation of
the first two of these treaties. Article 43 of the last one requires that a Lil-
itary- occupant must respect unless absolutely prevented the laws in force in the
country. It would appear that if Japan is regarded as a military occupant in
China she has no right to make the laws and pursue its practices of which we have
complained, discriminating against American nationals in this area.

=== Page 204 === (confidence 0.99)
THE AMERICAN COMMITTEE FOR NON-PARTICIPATION IN JAPANESE AGGRESSION
2.
It is my opinion that the imposition of the import discriminations suggested,
and also the imposition of an embargo prohibiting export to Japan of proclaimed
materials would be entirely legal and appropriate as a measure of reprisal, be-
cause of Japanese vlolation of her obligations under these treaties to which both
Japan and the United States are bound. I may say that this subject was discussed
in connection with Ethiopian sanctions and it was recognized that rights of Italy
under commercial treaties of the kind referred to, could legally be withheld by
states engaged in sanctions against Italy because of the fact that Italy had vio-
lated obligations under general treaties. You can find reference to this decision
of the legal subcommittee of the League of Nations Sanction Committee in an appen-
dix to a book which I edited entitled Neutrality and Collective Security, Harris
Foundation Lectures for l936, University of Chicago Press, p. 2l8. The report of
the subcommittee was as follows:
"A second question of an intcrnational character arises out of
the existence of commerclal treaties between Italy and the states
which participate in the sanctions. Application of sanctions
by a state having a commercial treaty with Italy may, to a greater
or lesser degree, prevent the execution of the treaty. Italy
would, however, have no legal right to complain, since the si-
tuation so created would be the result of the provisions of the
Covenant, which is legally binding on both Italy and the other
state and prevailed over the treaty in question."
While the present situation is not quite so clear because Japan is not bound
to the United States by a treaty providing specifically for economic sanctions,
yet I think in principle the general rule which permits a suspension of the
operation of commercial treaties as a measure of reprisal against a state which
has violated obligations under international la: would result in the same prin-
ciple being applicable.
Quincy Wright

=== Page 208 === (confidence 0.99)
FRED H. LYSONS
LAWYER
SEATTLE, WASH.
January 24, 1939.
Roger B. Greene, Esq., Chairman
Ameriean Committoe for Non-participation
in Japamese Aggression
8 Wost Portieth Street
New Tork, New York
My dear Sir:
The organization of your Committee offers a hopeful prospeot of
averting world wide disaster which would inevitably result from Japan's sub-
jugation of China.
China spoke the truth in 1928 when, in announcing her acceptance
of the Kellogg-Briand Peace Pact she characterized the Far East as the
world' danger spot. It has been such ever since, is today and will con-
tinue to be until Japan is made impotent in her destructive egotistical
ambitions of military and commercial worlc dominance.
Already her attack on China has unleashed the madmen of Europe,
for the Hitler and Mussolini moves were made possible by Japan's Far East
threat against Britain, France and Russia. These moves will continue to
their logioal conclusion unless these three mentioned nations are relieved
from this threat and restored to their customary place of balancing powers
in Europe.
You offer a commendable two point program of (1) arousing public
sentiment in support of (2) governmental executive action to stop supplies
to Japan.
You state that your appeal for public sentiment is to be made to
various groups "on grounds of both moral responsibility and far-sighted self-
interest", and that these groups "will be invited to lend support to all who
voluntarily saerifice a share in profits from war trade with Japan"; and you
speak of seeking legislation on the subject.
Noting your further statement that "Study is being made of the
forms of effective action possible under existing laws", permit me to sug-
gest a present available and completely effective means, which is not de-
pendent upon either voluntary or legislative action.
I refer to the Nine Power treaty, which obligates us:
(1) "To respect the sovereignty, the indepndence,
and the territorial and administrative integrity
of China."
By decisions of the Supreme Court of the United States a treaty:

=== Page 209 === (confidence 0.99)
"Stands on the same footing of supremaoy as do the pro-
visions of the Constitution and the laws of the United
States. It operates of itself, without the aid of any
legislation, state or national" Asakura v. City of
Seatt1•, 265 U.8. 332 (68 L Ed. 1041):
"Is to be construed in a broad and liberal spirit, and
where two constructions are possible, one restrictive
of rights that may be claimed under it and the other
favorable to them, the latter is to be preferred."
Asakura v. City of Seattle (supra).
"Should be interpreted in a spirit of"uberrima fides"
(syllabus) Tuoker v. Alaxandroff, 183 US. 424
"To be exeouted in the utmost good faith, with a view of
making effeotive the purpose of the high contracting
parties". Sullivan v. Kidd, 254 US 433-468.
"A construction of a treaty by the political department
of the government while not conolusive, . . . . . . . is
nevertheless of much weight." Charlton v. Kelly, 229 US
447-68; Su1livan v. Kidd, 254 US 433-442.
"Rights fixed by compact between nations, become con-
clusive upon all the subjects and citizens thereof."
Poole v. Fleeger, 11 Peters (US) 185-209.
It seems clear therefore that these permitted shipments of war
materials, with kmowledge, constitutes our own violation of the treaty;
makes us particeps criminis with Japan, as was well put by kt. Rev. G.
Ashton Oldham, in a letter to the New York Times. It is thus not merely
our right, but becomes our duty to stop them not because of anything Japan
is doing, but as a correction of our own misoonduct under the treaty.
And it is equally clear, under judicial interpretation of treaty
enforcement, that such endorsement may be by Executive action. Japan denies
our right to question her conduct in the Far East; says in effect that she
i8 minding her own business, and invites us to do the same.
Very well. If Japan may violate the treaty by affirmative assault
on China, free from our right to say her nay, a fortiori may not we comply
with the treaty by negative non-participation in her assault.
This therefore, is the theory on which we should act.
That it was the treaty intention to give right of action to any
one signatory alone is indicated by the language of Article VII:
"The contracting Powers agree that whenever a situation
arises which in the opinion of any one of them involves the
applioation of the stipulations of the present Treaty, and
renders desirable discussion of suchh application, there
shall be full and frank communication between the Contract-
ing Powers concerned."

=== Page 210 === (confidence 0.99)
-3
No joint action is contemplated -- merely oommunications and
this only under the correlative conditions connected by the oonjunotive
and. surely the time has long since passed when any accomplishment sould
 haped for through communication between the signatories.
With Europe embroiled, from whence is to come the salvation of
China, and the restoration of world tranquility? The opportunity for this
service is ours, and ours alone.
From our improvement of this opportunity -- and performance of
our duty as I view it -- will come material reward almost beyond computation.
China must necessarily be rehaoilitated, at cost running into the
billions. She will resume her program of internal improvement and moderniza-
tion projected and even undertaken shortly before Japan's assault -- billions
of dollars more.
Our thus beooming China's first friend in this her day of distress
will insure to us not alone this, but China's commercial and political
friendship for indefinite time to come.
I can appreciate President Roosevelt's concern lest a threat against
Japan might blanket the country with war fear. Limiting our action to regula-
tion of our own conduct merely, no such concern would arise. Contra, I can
think of no act of wider popular appeal than our withdrawal from a war in whioh
we are now actually engaged.
As to fear of retaliation from Japan resulting from such negative
action, think of the greater chance we took in this regard in affirmatively
advancing $25,000,000 to China in compliance with our further Nine Power
Treaty obligation --
"To provide the fullest and most unembarrassed oppor-
tunity to China to develop and maintain for herself an
offective and stable government."
Unanir. s public approval was the response to this act. Why should
different react!  be expect-d from merely squaring our own conscience by
halting these sup ies?
Regardle. ? what other nations may do, why should not we observe
our treaty obligatic. I In this day of treaty flouting and rulers running
riot what more appropriate and essential time for this great land of ours,
thi America, to set before the vorld its example of treaty respect and
orderly conduct?
Surely the certain resulting moral satisfaction to our souls from
 righteous conduct contributing to world well being, and the material gains
from a grateful people should promt us to immediate action.
And, this is important. With Hitler and Mussolini now apparently
released from their venture in Spain, who knows but that they may transfer
this aid to Japan?
Montion of Hitler trings to mind this feature of the Japanese situa-
tion which causes criticism on the Pacific Coast:

=== Page 211 === (confidence 0.98)
Great Britain and France are impotent to stop the Hitler outrages
against the Jews because of Japan's threat to their Far East interests
neeessitating their attention there. Japan has been kept going through
supplies from us, one of the essentials being sorap iron. The Jews, prin-
ipally, are profiting from this traffic. Doubtless Dr. Stephen S. Wise
and other Jewish members of your Committee will be able to promptly end
this.
I have reason to believe that prompt Executive action as herein
suggested awaits only the presentation of an appeal from an organization
listing such influential membership as yours. ISenator Schwellenbach from
this state, an administration confidant and advisor, favors it and believes
the treaty authorizes it.
I further have reason to believe that Mr. Farley's cooperation may
be had. My brother, J. W. Lyoons, of the Elk's Magasine, Now York, has had
the subject up with him, and thrcug their Elks fraternal relations may be
the appropriate porson to further the suhject.
May I be of aid in any additionel manner, particularly in the
direction of haste, which I regard as a primary requisite?
In any public reference to me you may identify me as member of
The Amorican Council, Institute of Pacific Relations.
Very truly yours,
Fred H. Lysons
Lowran Fuilding
Seattle, Wash.

Sales of war aterials by the United States to Japan during the first
•leven months, January-November, l938, reached a total value of apprarimately 142
mdllion dollars. This compares with 157 million dollars for the corresponding period
in l937, a decrease of approximately 15 million or 9.7 per cent. In the same period,
however, the value of all the United States' exports (including reerports) to Japan
declined 22.4 per cent ($60,896,000) or 12.7 per cent more than the percentage drop in
the value of war material exports [see Table 2 (B)]. If the United States exports to
Japan are grouped into three broad categories as in Table 2, (A) & (B), then it is
seen that as against the 9.7 per cent drop in the value of war material sales, the
value of raw cotton exports fell 2l.5 per cent while the value of exports other than
war materials and raw cotton went down 59.7 per cent.

The significant fact is that, whereas the ratio of war materials exports to
all exports during the January-November period was 57.8 per cent in l937, it rose to
1
The term "war materials" is used here to include not only arms, ammunition and
implements of war as defined under the present Neutrality Act, but also those
articles and materials directly or indirectly of use for war purposes.

=== Page 219 === (confidence 0.98)
67.3 per cent in 1938 [see Table 2, (A)]. In other words, of every $100.00 the United
States received from exports to Japan in the said period the sum of $67.30 was in pay-
ment for war materials in l938 compared with $57.80 in the previous year.
The most precipitous fall in the value of sales took place in iron and steel
semi-mamufactures, dropping from $70,297,000 to $30,080,000 or a reduction of
40,217,000. Sales of automobiles, parts and accessories declined from $13,334,000 to
$9,141,000 or a reduction of $4,193,000. These and other minor reductions, however,
were partially off-set by such substantial increases as $1,037,298 for ferro-alloys,
$1,188,596 for lead, $6,961,000 for petroleum and its products, $8,125,000 for air-
craft and parts, and $12,031,000 for metal-working machinery.
Particular attention is called to the phenomenal rise in the amount of
metal-working machinery supplied to Japan by the United States. This is of special
note in view of the admitted backwardness of Japan's heavy industries; such heavy im-
ports are apparently designed to overcome Japan's technical weakness in this respect
so as to provide an adequate basis for her armament industries. The strategic impor-
tance of the United States as a supplier of machinery equipments to Japan can hardly
be over-emphasized, because the United States not only makes machine tools of the
highest quality, but also because it is the only country at the present juncture that
1s in a position to deliver orders for machine tools within the short period of time
these are required by the Japanese. Nor can one lay too much importance upon the in-
creased sales of aircraft and parts, because these sales help provide the Japanese with
precisely those instruments of war, in the mamufacture of which Japan still lacks the
necessary skill and technical organization. Significant also is the increase in the
exports to Japan of petroleum and its products, in view of Japan's proverbially woe-
ful deficiency in oil for war purposes.
It should be pointed out that the decrease of approximately 15 million dol-
lars in the value of war materials exported by this country to Japan, already noted,
by no means implies a like decrease in physical volume. In fact, a cursory examination
of columms 8 and 9 of Table 1 will show that a drop in prices, not a decrease in
- 2 -

=== Page 220 === (confidence 0.98)
physical volume in some of the mjor items was responsible for the 15 million dollar
decrease. For example, under iron and steel semi-mamufactures, we note that the most
important item -- iron and steel scrap, including tinplate scrap and waste-waste tin-
plate -- declined 50.7% in value as against only 27.5% in quantity, so that the drop
of price for this 1tem accounted for about $9,084,000 of its decrease of $19,880,000
1
entered in columm 7, Table 1.
The same situation holds true for pig iron; steel ingots, blooms, billets
and slabs not containing alloy; and wire rods. Again, in the case of copper, for in-
stance, if the factor of price drop were eliminated, there would have been an increase
in value of about $4,574,000 instead of the decrease of $234,000 recorded in column 7,
Table 1.
The problem of making ad justments for price changes will be dealt with
fully in analyzing the annual figures. As regards the ll-month figures with which
we are here concerned, it suffices to point out that the total drop of 15 million
dollars in l938 can be entirely attributed to the drop of prices, which brought down
the total value of war material sales by somewhere in the neighborhood of 17 million
dollars. For the period compared, therefore, insofar as physical volume was con-
cerned there had been no decline in the flow of war materials from the United States
to Japan. There may have even been a slight increase in l938.

followed by metal-working machinery (15.1%); nonferrous metals (l4.4%); arms,
ammunition and implements of war (7.2%); automobiles, parts and accessories (6.4%);

The overwhelming proportion of the commodities listed here as essential for
war purposes do not come under the Neutrality Act. That Act concerns itself only with
arms, ammunition and implements of war, which constituted only l.3 per cent and 7.2
per cent respectively of all war materials sold by the United States to Japan, in the
eleven-months period of 1937 and 1938.

Dear Senator,
Is the Government doing anything to discourage the
sale of scrap iron, munitions, and gasoline to the Japanese
army?
It seems indeed a foolish policy to keep supplying the
tools that will nail, bar, and bolt the door to China and
Chinese trade.
I feel sure that you share my views on this matter,
but I want to relieve my conscience by telling you how I
feel. I hope you can spare a minute to read the enclosed
statement based on a lecture by Dr. Walton Judd lately
returned from China.
With warm congratulations on your election, which
cost me a day's pay (and at that was well worth it) I
remain
Yours sincerely,
CALEB WINSLOW

=== Page 229 === (confidence 0.89)
SUGGESTED PETITION TO BE SUBMITTED TO THE PRESIDENT OF THE UNITED
STATES ON THE JAPANESE INVASION OF CHINA
WHEREAS, the United States of Americe and many other nations have indicted
Jepen as the violator of the Nine-Power Treaty and the Kellog Pact, in the
present Sino-Japanese conflict, fomented by the Military Party of Japan, and
have repeatedly protested against the bombings of undefended Chinese cities
and the unnecessary slaughter of Chinese non-combatants, and
WHEREAS, we feel put to shame that American raw materials are being used to
menufacture weapons of war for Japan, American planes are being used to carry
Amorican-made bombs for the destruction of Chinese civilian population, American
shells are loading Japanese guns, and America is supplying to Japan huge quanti-
ties of scrap-iron, oil, trucks and other materials used in the furtherance of
the war, over and above the amounts of our normal trade of recent years, and
WHEREAS, we sympathize with the Chinese people in their desire to build up an
independent, democratic state, and end gravely threatened by the present aggression
of Japan, and deplore the traffic which makes it possible for certain American
interests to profit from the wanton destruction of the innocent victims of Japan-
ese aggression,
THEREFORE, we the undersigned respectfully petition the President of the United
States, in accordance with the principle of his Good Neighbor enunciation, and
in the interest of the people of the United States, China and Japan, to take
every practicable means, direct or indirect, to bring to an end a traffic from
our country which is compelling us to be a partner in the destruction of the
Chinese people.

470
On November 28/last you were so kind as to
894.24/614
write me with regard to the sale of munitions and war
materials to Japan.
I took the liberty of sending a copy of your
letter, in confidence, to a friend vho is much inter-
ested in this problem.
He now asks me this question:
Vould it be permissible for me to
pass on a cony of this letter to the American
Committee for Non-Participation in Japanese
Aggression and others who would be especially
interested?
I am in turn referring this question to you.
I take it that there is no technical reason which would
prevent my granting my friend's request; but my relations
with various members of the Department of State have been
and are so cordial that I should not wish to authorize
this use of your letter unless you should be willing to
have me do so.
Will you kindly let me know whether you would
be willing to have me authorize such use of your letter
as my friend has requested?
Yours sincerely,
Emoet H Wilkins

My dear Mr. Secretary,
rtt 
I have the honor to communicate to you the following resolutions unani-
894.24
mously adopted by the Trustees of the World Peace Founiation at a meeting of the
Board on February 11, 1939:
It was declared to be the opinion of the Trustees:
1. That the continuance of the supply to Japan of war materials from
the United States to enable Ja pan to carry out her war policy in China is in-
tolerable and contrary to the fundamental interests of the American people, and
that this country should prepare to make material sacrifices to bring about a
discontinuance of such supply.
2. That the cause of world peace will be served at the present time
894.24/617
by adequate naval and military preparedness by the United States.
3. That, in the light of the experience with the so-called neutrality
legislation of the past three years, the Trusteest declaration of October 19,
1935, which was as follows, should be reaffirmed:
That they favor legislation giving to the President large
discretion in effecting the neutrality of the United States
in the presence of international war.
That such discretion should extend to the use of embargoes
and generally the use of trade restrictions, and to the
articles of commerce to be included.
That the President should be free to use this discretion in
the protection of the interests of the United States, as ex-
pressed in our international engagements and as may in his
judgment most serve the cause of world peace and especially
the peace of the United States.
The World Peace Foundation believes that attempts to define
F/A
in advance and in detail the action of the United States by in-
flexible laws are ineffective and dangerous.

2..2/21/39.
It is the wish of the Trustees that you make whatever use of
these resolutions that you may deem advisable.
I am, my dear Mr. Secretary,
Sincerely yours,
S. Shepard Jones

=== Page 251 === (confidence 0.99)
JOHN F. LOGAN
Oregonian Building
Portland, Oregon
9 March 1939
Hon Charles L McNary
Senate Office Building
Washington, D. C.
My dear Senator:
We are having some considerable agitation in the
Pacific Northwest in the matter of loading scrap iron
bound for Japan--ultimately to be made into munitions
for all warfare uses and particularly for use in the
terrible undeclared war against China.
Mr. Joe P. Price and myself, representing the Chinese
peace societies and Chinese business men of Portland, have
been in association with Mr. Fred H. Lysons, a well known
lawyer of Seattle, representing the Chinese societies~ of
that city. Mr. Lysons came to Portland fully equipped
historically with the development of the Open Door, which
in turn was merged into what is known as the Nine Power
Treaty of l922, wherein all the signatories pledged them-
selves to maintain a certain status quo in the protection
of China against aggression by third persons or acts by her-
self--such as alienating her territory or giving special in-
terests to aggressors. This matter is set forth in a state-
ment which I am sending herewith enclosed.
The expression here in the Northwest is almost unanimously
in favor of the longshoremen refusing to handle this scrap iron--
which they have so far refused to do at Astoria, Marshfield,
and now Portland. The Press is full of commendatory articles,
particularly the issue of the Oregonian of this day, March 9th--
è portion of which I am enclosing.
Our immediate purpose is to make representations to the
State Department, particularly to Assistant Secretary Sumner
Welles, to give ear to the contention that allowing the ship-
ment of scrap iron by tnis country is a breach of the Nine Power
Treaty--in fact, it is our breach--and against the good
neighbor policy so often announced by this administration,
because, after notice, it is a refusal to carry out the full
intent

=== Page 252 === (confidence 0.99)
-2-
intent and purpose of the Nine Power Treaty. While we
know of no direct official statement of repudiation by
Japan, yet we know that their responsible statesmen have
said that the Nine Power Treaty was no longer in effeot
because of changed conditions.
I can say from personal knowledge that the sentiment
is almost universal among all sorts and conditions of men
that the government is not doing its full duty when it
extends a $25,000,000 credit to China in compl1ance with
Section 2 of Article 1 and then ruins the effect of that
by permitting supplies to go to Japan in violation of
Section l of that article.
Very truly yours,
JOHN F. LOGAN

Dear Mr. Welles:
On March 6th in the Bronxville Hih School we
heard a lecture on present day hina by r. Walter
Judd. He showeu moving pictures of the savagery
and brutality being inflicteu upon Chinese civilians -
especially helpless women and chiluren - by the
Japanese troops unuer the airection of the Japanese
Military Covernment.
Their aim is to break down the morale of the Chinese
894.24/622
Arany by mutilating those behin. the lines. We are
contributing to this slaughter by supplying Japan
with military sup lies and scrap iron. It is in our
power to stop this destruction of human life. If
every American could see these pictures and hear Dr.
Juda's lecture it woula brin; home to them the import-
ant part America is pl&ying in prolonging this war.
"e, being residents of Bronxville, N. Y., earnestly
urge o r government to put forward an effort to stop
the sale and shipment of all weapons, airplanes, tanks,
and other war materials to Japan, the aggressor nation
in the present Sino-Japanese conflict. This sho 1d be
cone as speecily as possible in oruer that the destruction
of human lives in China may no longer be the responsi-
bility and discredit of the government of the United
States.
Very truly yours,

I have asked Mr. Ward of Le his opinion
as to action which might be taken by the De-
partment in regard to the instances of picket-
ing referred to in the attaghed memorandum
and in regard to poasible ruture instances.
Mr. Ward replies that, as the instances
referred to in the memorandum appear to have
been already satisfactorily solved, there
would seem to be no reason for the Department
to conslder the taking of any action in re-
gard to them and, in fact, there 1s no action
which could properly be undertaken by the De-
partment as long as international law or
agreements are not infringed, which would
appear to be the case in the instances under
conslderation. As for future instences,
Mr. Ward states again that unless interna-
tional law or agreements should be infringed
there would be no appropriate action for
the Department to take.
I concur in Mr. Ward's opinions, and
I suggest that Mr. Ballantine be authorized
to exnress those opinions to Mr. Kase as on
his own responsibility 1f Mr. Kase should
again raise the question.

=== Page 262 === (confidence 0.99)
Coby a. Jokyo
rch 6, 1939
PICKET-OBSTRUCTION OF JAPANESE VESSELS IN OREGON
Feb. 23
The ship, NORWAY MARU of the Kawasaki Kisen Ka1sha
("K" Line), under charter to the Yamashita hipping
Co., calling at astoria, Oregon, to load 8o0 tons
of scrap iron, was picketed by about l00 Chinese,
who had received permission to picket from the mayor
of the city. The longshoremen struck in sympathy.
Feb. 25
The Japanese Consul at Portland made representations
to the authorities at Astoria and was told by them
that steps would be taken to end the picketing the
next morning (the 26th).
Feb. 25
At Coos Bay, Oregon, a vessel under charter to Mit-
sui & Co. was picketed by Chinese. The longshore-
men refused to load the vessel.
Feb. 27
The Japanese Consul at Portland approached Mr. John
Convill, City Manager of astoria, who replied that
inasmuch as the Chinese pickets were employin only
peaceful means of persuasion, he could do nothing
within the law.
March 1
The Consul received information from the municipal
authorities of Astoria, that as they had no juris-
diction over the longshoremen's association, the
matter had been referred to the Mstional labor Re-
lat fons' Board.
March 1
The Employers' Associe tion (Shipowners' Association?),
upon receiving authorization from their headquarters

Pioket-Obstruction of Japanese Vessels in Oregon
Feb. 23
The ship. NORWAY MARU of the Kawasaki Kisen Kaisha
("K" Line), under charter to the Yamashita Shipping
Co., calling at Astoria, Oregon, to load 8oo tons
of scrap-iron, was pieketed by about l00 Chinese,
who had received permission to picket from the mayor
of the city. The longshoremen struck in sympathy.
Feb. 25
The Japanese Consul at Portland made representations
to the authorities at Astoria and was told by them
that steps would be taken to end the picketing the
next morning (26th).
Feb. 25
At Coos Bay, Oregon, a vessel under charter to Mitsui
& Co. was pioketed by Chinese. The longshoremen re-
fused to load the vessel.
Feb. 27
The Japanese Consul at Portland approached Mr. John
Convill, city manager of Astoria, who replied that
inasmuch as the Chinese pickets were employing only
peaceful means of persuasion, he could do nothing
within the law.
March 1
The Consul received information from the municipal
authorities of Astoria, that as they had no jurisdice
tion over the longshoremen's association, the matter
had been referred to the National Labor Relations'
Board.
March 1
The Employers' Association (Shipowners' Association?),
upon receiving authorization from their headquarters
in San Francisco, decided to close the ports of
Astoria, Coos Bay, Wauna, and Warrenton, and sent a
delegation to Astoria as a demonstration.
March 1
A delegation of Chinese called on the Mayor of Port-
land, Mr. Carson, asking permission to picket two
Japanese vessels. the UKIN MARU and the HAKUBA MARU
(both owned by the Mitsui Line) which were due to
arrive in Portland to load scrap-iron on March 5th and
15th. respectively. The Mayor is said to have told
the delegation that he could not forbid the picketing.
The Consul sent a memorandum to the Governor of Ore-
gon, Mr. Sprague, requesting a prompt and satisfactory
sottlement of the oases and asking him to use effective
-1-

=== Page 266 === (confidence 0.98)
methods to prevent a recurrence of such situations
in the state.
aroh 8
The captain of the NORWAY-MARU reported to the
Japanese Consul that in the afternoon on March
ed, when food-stuffs from a local grocery store in
Astoria were being delivered to the ship, the same
group of Chinese who were "demonstrating,"
met the delivery man at the entrance of the dock
and prevented him from taking the food aboard the
ship. Some members of the crew, in order to get
the supplies, were obliged to leave the ship and
take the food from the deliveryman.
The captain has, therefore, asked the Consul
to request the American authorities to regulate
this unjustified action on the part of the "demon-
strators."
March 3
The Japanese Consul in Portland requested the Mayor
of Astoria, Dr. Logan, to take effective and proper
measures to let the Japanese maintain their trade
and security of living saying that not only was a
"demonstration" which interrupted lawful transac-
tions in itself unjustifiable as well as harmful to
Japanese-American trade relations, but was far from
being a mere "peaceful demonstration" when it checked
the loading of food-stuffs for the use of the ship's
personnel.
March 4
The Mayor of Astoria replied by telephone to the
Consul that he had taken the necessary steps as re-
quested by the Consul..
March 4
The captain of the NORWAY-MARU also reported to the
Consul that the Chinese pickets had been disbanded
ard that the longshoremen had begun loading the
cargo.
Maroh 4
When the Greek ship, Ann Strathats, entered Portland
to take on 8oo0 tons of scrap iron bought by Mitsui,
about 750 Chinese people made a demonstration such
as the picket in Astoria. The longshoremen struck
in sympathy.

=== Page 267 === (confidence 0.99)
11
It was learned today that the Port Commission
of Astoria of which the City Manager is a member
passed a resolution as follows:
Whereas the matter of the handling of
scrap iron was brought to the attention of
the board by the manager and the difficulty
that has been experienced in the case of
the S. S. Norway Maru and the fact that the
storage and handling of scrap iron on the
port property is especially detrimental and
causes a greater damage to the property
than other commodities and furthermore that
the space is required for the s torage of
boats and ot her commodities;
Now Therefore be it resolved that the
manager be and he is hereby instructed to
discontinue the acceptance of scrap iron
for storage and shipment from the port
docks excepting only the present shipment
in question.
March 11
(Astoria) The Arbitrator made a decision that the
longshoremen violated their contract with the
Employers' Association, on the ground that they
(the longshoremen) refused to load the scrap iron
aboard the ship, even though the Chinese demonstra-
tion did not entail any possibility of danger or
injury for them.
March 12
S. S. Kostis, under charter to Mitsubishi & Co. and
S. S. Uyo Maru are going to leave Portland on March
13 and 15 respectively.
March 14
S. S. Kostis, under charter to Mitubisi & Co. arrived
at Portland from Coos Bay and failed to load scrap
iron as the longshoremen refused to work on account
of the Chinese pickets.
March 14
The municipal authorities of Portland told the Japa-
nese Consul there that these troubles arising from
the Chinese picket which was hampering the Japanese-
American trade should be dealt with by the Federal
Government.

March 15, 1939.
FEL
Senator MoNary and told him that I had just
disoussed with Mr. Welles the matter which
Senator MoNary had referred to me this
morning (see attached memoranda) and that
Mr. Welles had asked me to say to the Senator
that, after having considered the matter very
carefully and with a sincere desire to be
helpful, we had come to the conclusion that
it would not be good strategy for the Depart-
ment of State to injeot itself into the
Portland situation; that the Labor Depart-
ment's representative at Portland had sug-
gested that a telegram from a governmental
source stating that there is no federal law
prohibiting export of scrap iron to any
foreign country might be of help to the local
authorities; that in as much as there had
been raised the question of a federal statute
it seemed to us that a statement in regard to
such a matter should come from the Department
of Justice. I emphasized that we desired to
be of all practicable help but that we were
convinoed, on the basis of our present under-
standing of the situation, that it would be
the opposite of helpful were this Department
to volunteer any statement in relation to
the matter under consideration. The Senator
first indicated that he did not see why the
Department of Justice should issue any state-
ment on the point mentioned and I explained
that

=== Page 269 === (confidence 0.98)
DEPARTMENT OF STATE
DIVISION OF FAR EASTERN AFFAIRS
- 2 -
that in as much as a question of federal
law was involved, it would seem that the
Department of Justice would be the appro-
priate department of the Government to
express a view as to whether there was
any legal prohibition applying to exports
of sorap iron. I suggested that the
Senator might wish to take the matter up
with the Department of Justice and I of-
fered, if the Senator so desired, to tele-
phone the Department of Labor and suggest
that if the Department of Labor wished to
obtain a statement on the question whether
there was any federal statute prohibiting
the export of scrap iron the Department of
Labor might care to approach the Department
of Justice. The Senator, however, did not
ask me to carry out the offer but com-
mented that his understanding of what I
had said was that the State Department did
not wish to take any action in the matter.
I replied that this was correct and that
we felt that the taking of an initiative
by this Department would be the opposite
of helpful.
