FAR EASTERN ALTAIRS
My dear Mr. Secretary:
letter from the Chinese American Citizens Alliance, 1044 Stockton Street, San
Francisco:
894.24/626
"This organization, composed of American citizens of Chinese descent
throughout the United States, respectfully calls your attention to the
894.2
horrible conditions existing in China created by the undeclared war on the
part of Japan, and urges that you initiate or sup ort immediately effective
governmental measures designed to prohibit further credit to and snipment
of arms, munitions and materials of war to Japan. In utter disregard of the
sanctity of treaties and in pursuance of its policy of aggression, Japan
is bringing chaos and unspeakable horror to China, and threatens to do more
damage unless effectively stopped by action or America in denying to Japan
the essential materials wherewith to wage this undeclared war.
Undefended cities have been and are being continuously bombed,
property wantonly destroyed, merely to satisfy the lust of destroying, and
millions of innocent people left homeless and faced with starvation. In
addition, the fighting forces of China have suffered a million casualties,
including the killed and wounded. Vast territories are being occupied by
the invader, which drives sixty million civilians from their homes. China
is bleeding to death.
Millions of American money have been sent to China to relieve the
sick, the wounded and the starving. On the other hand, America continues
to sell to Japan war materials and scrap iron with which to make bullets to
kill these same people for whom Americans so generously contributed money
to help. It all seems so illogical and senseless. We believe that once
the American sense of fair play is aroused, she will never allow herself to
become a partner with Japan in the latter's murderous adventure to conquer
China. China is bleeding to death. Immediate action is needed to save her.
Japan is able to carry on this inconceivable barbarity because
F/FG

=== Page 277 === (confidence 0.97)
- 2 -
The Honorable Secretary of State
American war material is made available to her. Therefore, we urge you
and the members of Congress to take every practical means to end the traffic
in war materials to Japan, thus preventing further bloodshed and needless
sacrifice of human lives."
I will appreciate any statement you may care to make to me as to the
attitude of the State Department toward this protest.
Sincerely yours,

894.24/627
I have the pleasure of sending you a copy of the

to you and to the Division of Far Lastern Affairs.

=== Page 282 === (confidence 1.00)
RESCLUTIONS
The following resolutions were adopted by the Trustees of the
World Peace Foundation at the annual meeting of the Board on February 1l,
1939:
"It was declared to be the opinion of the Trustees:
1. That the cont inuance of the supply to Japan of war materials
from the United States to enable Japan to carry out her war policy in China
is intolerable and contrary to the fundamental interests of the American
people, and that this country should prepare to make material sacrifices
to bring about a discontinuance of such supply.
2. That the cause of world peace will be served at the present
time by adequate naval and military preparedness by the United States.
3. That, in the light of the experience with the so-called
neutrality legislation of the past three years, the Trustees' declaration
of otober 19, 1935, which was as follows, should be reaffirmed:
That they favor legislation giving to the President large
discretion in effecting the neutrality of the United States in
the presence of international war.
That such discretion should extend to the use of embargoes
and generally the use of trade restrictions, and to the articles
of commerce to be included.
That the President should be free to use this discretion in
the protection of the interests of the United States, as expressed
in our international engagements and as may in his judgment most
serve the cause of world peace and especially the peace of the
United States.
The World Peace Foundation believes that attempts to define
in advance and in detail the action of the United States by in-
flexible laws are ineffective and dangerous."

March 231989

Dear Sir:-- I am enclosing for your perusal signed pe-
titions dealing with the Jananese aggression situation.
I find the people of this community universally opposed
to any action upon the part of American commerce which
aids Japan 1n her war upon China. I have never had anyone
to refuse to sign this petition. Most of these signature
were taken in meetings conducted by our church.
Sincerely yours,

=== Page 293 === (confidence 0.98)
Whereas American alrolanes, gasoline, and munitions
are used by Japan for the bombardment of Chinese cities, and
the consequent slaughter of 1nnocent people, and for the
destruotion of the only means of subsistence of millions in
China,
Whereas this policy of selling munitions to Japan 1s a
ruthless making of profit from the blood and tears of mil-
lions of innocent people, and when pursued while we protest
against Japanese ruthleseness 1s 1nconsistent and Pharisaical
on our part.
Be 1t Resolved:-That we urge upon the Government of the
United States to put into affect a "Policy of Non-Participa-
tion 1n Agression" by stopning the shipment to agressor na-
tions of all goods that can be used by their military forces,
and that 1mmediate steps be taken to stop their shipment to
Japan.
Also to urge that any measures possible for a peace-
loving people be taken to aid China in its desperate resist-
ance against the invaders.
SIGNATURES:-
(This 1s a copy of the petition which accom-
panied the letter of Apr1l 4, 1939, from
Representative Harter of Ohio. It contained
106 signatures.)

Dear Mr. Secretary:
partent of State
I enclose letter published in the N. Y. Sunday
OF
Times of March 19 in which I have urged that we should
stop supplying iron and steel and other war materials to
Japan which she is using to prepare herself for an attack
upon us.
We all know there is (or has been) great opposi-
tion to our participation in any foreign war; but no one
can oppose our stopping the shipment from our shores of
894.24/634
materials to make Japan strong enough to defy and dictate
to us.
We all stand by in horror at Germany's rapidly
mounting power and the way she is using it. Japan is the
Germany of the Far East. We have the power now to stop
her career of death-dealing destruction without resort
to war. If this issue were properly understood by the
American people, Japan's career of conquest would be brought
to a quick close.
894.24
Very truly yours,

TAYG 28 1941
DEPARTMENT OF STATE
CHARACTER OF SCRAP EXPORTED IN 1937
1322
Of the 4,039,143 tons of scrap exported last year,
934,065 tons consisted of No. 1 heavy melting scrap of
suitable quality for use in our steel industry. Of about
2,510,654 tons consisted of No. 2 heavy melting of which
perhaps one-half was of suitable grade for use in our steel
mills. Of about 317,373 tons were No. 2 cast and 277,041
tons were of No. 2 bundles and miscellaneous ecrap which
has little or no demand in this country. Thus, about 54%
of our total exports under other circumstances might have
894.24/635
found use in this country, while 46% was of much less
value here than in export markets.
(Dictated over the telephone by
Walter A. Janssen, Bureau of
2
Foreign and Domestic Commerce,
Department of Commerce. 3/21/39)
AUG 29 1941

SOME PRO AND CON CONSIDEHATIONS WITH R SPECT TO THE
PROPOSED RESTRICTION OF EXPORTS OF SCRAP IRON
AND STEEL.
PRO:
1. The great expansion of exports during the past
few years to Japan, Italy and Germany.
2. The rapidly arising proportion of total exports
of orap going to these countries.
3. The unquestioned effect that these supplies of
scrap have been of material aseietance in stepring up
the armaments industries in these countries.
4. The fact that the United States is the prinoipal
large soale market for such sorap; in some cases there
has been no surplue for export due to heavy domestio
demand and in other cases legislative restriotions have
been imposed on exports.
CON:
1. A prohibition or radical restriotion of the
exportation of sorap iron might prove to be of some
embarrassment

=== Page 304 === (confidence 0.98)
-2-
embarrassment to Japan, Italy and Germany but it 1s not
probable that their armament industries would be crip-
pled by this action alone. It 1s probable that there
would be little serious effect on the industries in
Italy and Germany. In the oase of Japan, American scrap
has forued one of the principal raw materials for the
1ron and steel industries. It is probable, however,
that if this resource were cut off Japan vould be able
to turn to other sources of basic materials for her in-
dustry. To a great extent sorap iron and pig iron are
interohangeable in the manufacture of steel and it 1s
probable that Japan's first recourse, if scrap iron sup-
plies are cut off, would be tc incresse the importation
of pig iron. The Japanese industry already imports very
large amounts of pig iron from abroad, including during
the last two yeare very heavy sup. lies from the United
States and unles: these foreign souroes of pig iron were
cut off at the same time that shipments of scrap iron
were prohibited or restricted, the Jananese industry
would be embarrassed to the extent that slightly higher
expenditures might be required for pig iron. Should
foreign souroes of pig iron be restrioted at the same
time then it is probable that Japan would seek to

m
I nave learned recently from unverified sources
that a large petroleum refinery on the Pacific Coast, pre-
sumably Shell,is manufacturing dichloro diethylene sulfide
(commonly called mustard gas) from ethylene produced in its
cracking stills, and that this war gas is being exported to

the facts regarding these rumors; and if the Department has not
been appraised of the rumor, it might be interested in investi-
gating the story.
For the above reasons, I am passing it along to the
Department for what it may be worth.

=== Page 321 === (confidence 0.96)
- 2 -
It should be noted that the figures given above in-
clude consumption only by mills of the Japan Cotton
Spinners Association. Some cotton is consumed in other
establishments and in households.
The foregoing table shows that during the period
covered an average of 95 per cent of Japanese cotton im-
ports was consumed by mills of the Japan Cotton Spinners
Association and that of the cotton consumed in 1936 and
1937 by those mills 5l per cent went into cotton manu-
factures exported. Thus the percentage of Japan's imports
of cotton which were used for Japanese military purposes
during the period covered could not have exceeded 49 per
cent of the total imports. A rough computation for the
first eleven months of 1938 gives 1,340,000 bales of
cotton exported in the form of cotton manufactures as
compared with 2,330,000 bales of raw cotton imported.
Thus, as 57 per cent of the imports of raw cotton during
this period was converted into cotton manu actures which
were exported, not more than 43 per cent o: the cotton
imported during the first eleven months of 19s8 could have
been used for military purposes.

April 6th, 1939
Hon. James J. Davis
The United States Senate,
Washington, D. C.
My dear Senator Davis:
I understand that added airplanes have been sent
to Japan during the past week. Isn't it high time that
Congress should deal with that situation? Circumstances
have made us the chief abettor of Japanese aggression
against China, quite contrary to the wishes of the
American people. We ought to have stopped that trade
months ago.
And what I want 1s not the Pittman "cash and carry"
but a complete embargo against all war materials to
aggressor nations. I am convinced that popular sentiment
for that policy 1s overwhelming, and I hope you can find
it possible to support it. We re surely big enough to
stop that trade and to do it without getting hurt.
Sincerely yours,
IRWIN R. BEILER
Ch'mn of the Division of Philosophy
and Religion in Allegheny College

you on the subjeot of the sale of war munitions and materials to
Japan, and I realize that you must already know the faots even
more fully than I do, yet I am con strained to add my humble word
of appeal, feeling that only by so doing I can have rest of oon-
science.
I have recently retuned from an offioinl
visit to China, where I formerly labored as a missionary for many
years. The China Inland Mission, which I represent, is much the
largest of all Missionary Societies working there, having more
than 1,300 missionaries, over 300 of whom are from the U.S.A.
C3
The soenes I witnessed of devastation, death, suffering and destitution -
6
all the oruel result of the unwarrantable invasion of Japan - baffle
4.24/642
desoription and fill me with horror and indignation. Japan is guilty
of perpetrating in China one of the blaokest orimes in all human
history. I find the sentiment here at home one of almost universal
sympathy for China and condennation of Japan. Yet, notwithstanding
this, the U.S.A. is making herself a guilty partner of Japan by
supplying that nation with a large proportion of the materials
essential to carrying on this war of ruthless aggression and whole-
sale slaughter. It has been repeatedly stated by those who are in
a position to know the facts that without this assistance from Amerioa
Japan could not possibly have continued the war to the present time.
Nor oould any other nation furnish her adequately with the basio war
materials which America is supplying.
I cannot but raise my voice,along with many
others who have al ready spoken, in earnest protest against suoh an
unholy allianoe on the part of a God-fearing and justioe-loving nation
FOLED
like America. While I speak primarily out of heartfelt sympathy and
deep concern for suffering China, I have in mind also the very grave
ultimate bearing of tis oonfliot upon America itself. By her con-
tinued support of Japan's mad military ambition, the U.S.A. is help-
ing to set up a powerful aggressor State whioh unquestionably will not
only stifle Amerioan trade in the Far East but will in turn become a
grave menace to this country's peace and safety on her Pacifio borders.
For more than one vital reason, therefore, it is
F/A
of the utmost importance that measures be taken, and taken promptly, to
put a stop to this iniquitous partnerahip with Japan in the inhuman
course of action she is pursuing. In taking steps oonsistently and

Memorandum of Conversation

File

Mr. Pierrepont Moffat, Chief, Division of European Affairs

MR. HORNBECK
APR 281939
Mav I
DEPARTMENT OF STATE
1—1408
I asked Mr. Mallet, Counselor of the British
Embassy, to call this afternoon and explained that
we have received information from Paris to the effect
that since the seizure of the Spratley Islands France
APR92 1939
has cut off all deliveries of iron from French Indo-
China to Japan. The French, however, are upset about
this as it is seriously affecting the finances of Indo-
China, and the Japanese are meanwhile obtaining the
APB 99 1939
necessary 1ron from the Malay Peninsula. The French,
therefore, suggested to the British Government that
this source of supply be cut off. The British appar-
ently replied that they didn't see any use in cutting
off
11

=== Page 347 === (confidence 0.98)
1517
-2
off iron exports from British or French possessions so
long as Japan could obtain all the supplies of iron she
might need from the United States. The French understood
that the British were about to ask the U.S.A. if something
could not be done to cut off supplies of iron from the
United States to Japan.
The purpose in asking Mr. Mallet to call was to
suggest to him that 1t might be inadvisable for the
British to make such an approach. In the first place,
we would have to reply that the matter would receive
study from the point of view of our own independent
interests; that we had always pursued an independent
course, which though it often happened to parallel the
course of other Powers, could not be in the nature of
joint action. More important, however, was the fact
that if, as seemed probable, legislation looking toward
an embargo of certain types of steel and iron scrap
were introduced by Senator Pittmann it would be helpful
to this Department to be able to say that it had not
been approached on the matter by Great Britain or any
other foreign power.
Mr. Mallet replied that he had heard nothing what-
soever about the matter, and was inclined to doubt whether
the British were planning to make us this request. How-
ever, in view of the intimation I had just given him, he
woula

=== Page 348 === (confidence 0.90)
15」
-3-
would see that it were stopped, as the last thing the
British Government wished to do at the moment was to
074
embarrass us in any way.
Pierrepont Moffat

AIR MAIL POUCH
The Honorable
The Seoretary of State,
Vashington, D. C.
Sir:
I have the honor to inform the Department that
Dr. Roy B. Nutter, an Amerioan physioian who has re-
sided in Honduras for many years and has various
business conneotions in this oountry, called at this
mission a few days ago to make inquiry regarding the
possibility of selling mahogany to the United States
Government for use in making rifle stooks.
Dr. Nutter said that he is outting mahogany on
the North Coast of Honduras and has found that short
logs unfit for other purposes may be out into blocks
suitable

=== Page 363 === (confidence 0.98)
- 8 -
suitable for the manufacture of stooks for rirles. He
added that Señor Ignacio Aguroia, honorary Japanese
Consul at Teguoigalpa, has informed him that these
blooks of wood oould be sold in Japan, wheroupon Dr.
Nutter offered to supply 250,000 blooks of mahogany,
measuring approximately 2 feet in length and contain-
ing about one board foot, at 25 oents United States
ourrenoy eaoh, but he ha- thus far received no reply
to his offer. Dr. Nutter stated further that he oan
out the 250,000 blooks in a period of six weeks.
Dr. Nutter was advised to communioate direot with
the Springrield Armory, Springfield, Massaohusetts,
as well as with other manufecturers of rifles in the
United States.
Rospeotfully yours,
For the Minister:
Fred K. Salter

144.24/634
Thank you for your letter of March 31 in re the
proposal to curb the supply of materials and munitions to
Japan.
I understand that Senator Pittman is to intro-
duce a bill to curb Japan's trade with us and I enclose
copy of a letter which I am sending to him.
To avoid the complications resulting from the
damnable Neutrality law, Congress can enact a practically
prohibitive tariff on trade with Japan. We have a perfect
legal and moral right to do this for our own protection.
We really have the game in our own hands if we
have intelligence enough to take advantage of the situa-
tion before it is too late.
Needless to say we wore delighted with your
F/WB
tariff action against Germany.

=== Page 370 === (confidence 0.98)
Apr11 35, 1939
Hon. Key Pittman
U. S. Senate, Washington,
D. C.
Dear Senator Pittman:
I learn you are about to introduce a special bill to
ourb war trade with Japan. If this bill is properly framed and
pushed it should command almost unanimous support in Congress and
in the country.
Everyone favors strengthening our National defenses.
We can accomplish ten times as much for our safety by stopping
the flow of our raw materials and war materials to Japan as we can
by building battleships and fortifioations on our Pacific front.
We have a perfeot right to raise our tariffe on exports
to Japan and imports from Japan, to such an extent as we please,
and Japan cannot help herself now. But if we sit still and keep
on furnishing Japan with the materials for her great war machine,
the time may come and mot long henoe when Japan may be able to
shut off our supply of rubber and tin from the East Indies and to
tell us to repeal our Japanese exelusion act or our tariffs to
proteet our mamufacturers from the competition of goods made with
Japanese and Chinese cheap labor. It will mean a real war for
us then unless we are willing to yield to Japanese dictation and
that is unthinkable.
Let us aot now for our own protection, while we can
dietate to Japan and not wait till Japan is strong enough to dio-
tate to us.
This matter is confused in the public mind by the idea
that we must be "neutral". It is ten times as important that we
set in our own defenge se to be noutrel.
We ought to ourb Japan for our own proteotion even if
she were not attempting to conquer China. Fortunately for us
Japan has by her acts and her own publio statements revealed her
plams for wide conquest. Fortunately for us, too, China's defence
has so weakened Japan that she cannot now attack us, and we are
free to stop her great military mechine which ie sacrificing the
people of both China and Japan.
"Forewarned is forearmed." Japan has given us warning

=== Page 371 === (confidence 0.95)
.Key Plttman
-2
Apr11 28. 1922
emough of her plans for conquest. what are we going to do about
187
We have it in our power so to eurb Japen that we will
no longer need to spend billions on eur Pacifio Coast fleet and
fortifications and oan use our strength to protect our Atlantie
frontier.
Very truly youre,
CWB-R

Apr11 29, 1939.
Hon. Lyndon Johnson,
House of Representatives,
Washington, D. C.
Dear Mr. Johnson:--
I have read with indignation and shame for my coun-
try that Amerioa is supplying something like fifty peroent
of the war material with whioh Japan 1s slaughtering the
innocent people of China. What a travesty upon our pro-
fessions of friendship for China, and upon the denunoiation
of aggressor nations from the Cabinet and White House!
An American dootor recently returned from China says:
"The Chinese could save their country and their freedom if
only against Japan, but it 1s very doubtful if they can
against Japan and Amerioa. On the night the Japanese
ocoupied the city where I lived, I was more ashamed of my
country than ever before in my life. During the next few
days I removed a sugar sack full of Amerioan scrap iron
and shell fragments from the flesh of the Chinese wounded.
The Japanese were using four thousand to five thousand
Amerioan motor truoks in Shangha1 province, enabling them
to out-maneuver the Chinese troops entirely."
It certainly 1s a disgrace that any nation claiming
to be civilized should be a party to sucn crimes just in
order that a few of its oitizens may make money. No
wonder it 1s said that the almighty dollar is the god of
Amerioa. Cannot you people in Congress do something to
remove this stigma from our country's good name?
Trusting that you will use your utmost influence to
remedy this disgraceful and deplorable situation, I am,
with regards and very best wishes,
Yours very truly,
R. M. PERRENOT

With reference to the last para-
graph of the telegram from Paris
no. 874, May 3, 5 p.m., in which there
is indicated the possibility that the
French Government may approach us with
a view to the possibility of this Gov-
ernment's cutting off supplies of iron
ore from the thilippine Islands to
Japan, it might be desirable to instruct
Paris to endeavor to discourage such an
approach by inviting attention in appro-
priate quarters to the considerations
mentioned by Mr. Moffat to r. Mallet,
Counselor of the British Embassy, in
a conversation of April 19, 1939.
In a subsequent conversation on
April 29, Mr. Mallet indicated to Mr.
Moffat that the British had success-
fully discouraged the idea and that we
would hear nothing more about it. How-
ever, Mr. Bullitt's telegram presumably
refers to a later situation.

versation referred to are attached.

My dear Mr. Hull:
84424
We have the honor to present a broad picture of national protest
against America's further participation in Japan's illegal war on
China. A total of 620 petitions, or record of such, from all parts
of the country are being today mailed to Congressmen, from the
states represented, who are most interested in our foreign relations
policy.
We feel that as the Secretary of State you would wish to know that
these 620 petitions — only a small part of the total protest --
contain approximately 39l,000 signatures. In addition there is the
official action of unknown hundreds of thousands of members of 78
large and influential groups such as the Seattle Council of Churches,
Commonwealth Club of Minneapolis and the Council of U. S. Veterans.
Our Honorary Chairman Colonel Henry L. Stimson has pointed out Japan's
relationship to the Rome-Berlin Axis and their combined threat to the
future security of our way of life. It seems a shortsighted policy
to help build the power of an aggressor nation which has already re-
pudiated the terms of the Nine Power Pact and cast its lot with other
aggressors.
We urge full support of the Resolution of Representative John M.
Coffee placing an embargo on war shipments to Japan. Furthermore
we strongly recommend that the Committee not give final support to
The Peace Act of 1939 (S.J.57) usually referred to as the Pittman
"cash and carry" Resolution, unless the Far East is excluded from
F/WB
its provisions and unless special legislation is also provided to
check Japan's extensive purchases of war materials from the United
States.

ANCIENT AND ACCEPTED SCOTTISH RITE OF

May 9, 1939.
844.24
Honorable Cordell Hull,
Secretary of State,
Washington, D. C.
894.24/658
My dear Mr. Secretary:
As an individual citizen of the United States and one
deeply interested in its welfare, I desire to appeal to you in
behalf of justice, right and truth.
I was proud of the United States when it entered the
World Var, especially so because it was not actuated by any de-
sire for gain in the way of territory, finance or trade, but
purely from an altruistic standpoint. It appealed to me as the
act of a good samaritan, following the example described in the
Book of Books.
It is not our fault that ngland and France failed to
keep abreast of the conditions in surope in realizing what was
being done by dictator countries.
I am strong for aiding the small and weak nations. an
I sincerely believe that if the "nited States had given the least
intimation to Great Britain and France of even moral support, the
debacle at Munich would never happened and the scare of war would
have been dissipated entirely.
The fact that we lost fifty thousand soldiers in the orld
War is stressed almost daily, yet every year nearly that many of
our people are killed by automobiles. A large percentage of thos
who lost their lives in the World War were not killed, but died o
disease, and if they had not been in the service many of them would
have died anyway.
It is not such a terrible end for one to die for his coun-
try and for the benefit of humanity. The death of martyrs in the
past has often been of great benefit for generations afterwards.
I volunteered in the Spanish-American War and was captain
in the First Kentucky Volunteer Infantry. I am rather proud of
that fact, and I am willing to serve my country again in any ca-
pacity for which I am fitted to aid those countries which are now
or are about to be under the heels of despots.

=== Page 400 === (confidence 0.96)
-2-
I wonder where we would be today if Germany had won
in the Vorld Var. I wonder where we would be today if France
had not aided us in 1776. Nor must we forget how England stood
by us in approval of the Monroe Doctrine and with Dewey at
Manila.
I shall be proud of my country again when it takes a
firm stand for what is just and right in the situation overseas -
and I mean just and right from a world standpoint; not from a
standpoint of selfish isolation.
I am therefore strong for the Coffee Bill (H. R. 5432).
I believe that the Coffee Bill is the most satisfactory one so
far that has been introduced.
However the Pittman Resolution (3. J. 123), if amended
so as to exclude Japan, and the Pittman Resolution (s. J. 97),
known as the Peace Act of 1939, or "Cash and Carry" Act, if changed
regarding the situation in te Pacific so that China would be as-
sisted instead of Japan, they might answer the purpose.
Yours sincerely,
John H. Lovex
JHC*TN
4

May 9, 1939
The Hon. Franklin D. Roosevelt
ALLORDS
The White House, Washington
My dear Mr. President:
This is just a line to say for myself
and many of my friends that we are earnestly hoping that the
present iniquitous trade in war materials to Japan will be
stopped. The traffic seems to us both immoral and dangerous
to ourselves; and I hope very much that the new Pittman Reso-
C)
94.24/660
lution (S.J. Res. 123) and the Coffee Bil1 (H.R. 5432) will
be passed.
May I also take occasion to express the
hope that our unfortunate Neutrality situation will be changed.
I should very much myself like to see the Thomas Amendment
passed; and, if that is not possible, then the Pittman Cash
and Carry Resolution. Anythig will be better than the pre-
0
sent situation, which to my mind is both immoral and dangerous
as well as unworthy a great nation.
I am increasingly convinced that the only
way to keep America out of war is to keep war out of the world
and, therefore, feel that a change in our Neutrality law along
the lines above suggested is not only more worthy of this coun-
try and more in accord with moral principles, but also more
likely to keep America out of war than our present escapist,
isolationist policy.
The above sentiments are not only my own,
but represent many or most of my clergy, many of my people and
my friends in this vicinity; and I earnestly trust you will
give them your most sympathetic consideration.
Faithfully yours,
Cialtmblahang
F/WB
GAO/EBG
Eishop of Albany

My dear Mr. Secretary:
it ul Stau
642
I beg to acknowl6age receipt of a letter of
April 20 from the Department of State in reply to my letter of
April 13.
I am glad to be informed of the attitude of
the U.S. Government in not having issued during the past three
894.24/66
months any licenses for the export to Japan of arms, ammunitions,
or implements of war, including airoraft. But, as you are doubt-
less well aware, Japan's war purposes are still being well served
by the sale to her from America ofother materials direotly con-
tributory to the same end through their being used in the manu-
facture of implements of war in her own well equipped Munition-
Industrial plants.
I recognize the difficulties which the Neutrality
Act of May 1, 1937, imposes upon efforts on the part of the U.S.A.
to curb Japan's wicked oampaign of aggression in China, inasmuch as
that Aot does not permit of discrimination between the aggressor
nation and the victimized nation in the matter of the sale of war
materials. For this reason I am now writing again to add my earnest
plea for the support of such new legislation as thet proposed in
the new Pittman Resolution, and the Coffee Bill, now before the
Senate Foreign Relations Committee, and to urge all possible dis-
patch in putting through and giving effect to these measures, as
designed to put a stop to Japan's iniquitous doings in China which
not only threaten the destruotion of that great and worthy nation,
but which also vitally affect the safety and rights of American
citizens in the Far East, and which if allowed to continue un-
checked must inevitably become a grave menace to this country's
peace and safety.
F/WB
I would as strongly urge against support of the
Peace Act of 1939, unless the Far East is excluded from its provis ions,
and unless special legislation is also provided to stop Japan's pur-
chase of war materials in the U.S.A.
Believe me,
Very respeotfully yours,
Roberttallflover
HOME DIRECTOR

MAY 22 1939
My dear Secretary Hull:--
Depariment of State
894.24/663
I continue to receive letters almost every day pro-
testing against the attitude taken by our Government with respect to
the Sino-Japanese war situation based on the assumption that the United
States is giving Japan an advantage by selling raw materials to them
which makes it possible for them to make their own bombs and other war
equipment. Today, for example, I received a letter from George E.
Lerrigo, a Kansan who for several years has been a missionary in China.
I quote from his letter:
"Won't you support the amendment which will make it illegal for
America to continue to export war munitions to any nation which
will not respect treaties. This would put an end to our helping
Japan to the extent of 54% of the materials which she uses in
bombing the population of China. That recent news 'Japan Bombs
Chunking' which announced the raid of 32 planes on that capital
city and cost 3,000 lives, should have been headed 'America Aids
Japan to Bombard Chunking'. This will continue to be so until our
Congress acts and puts an end to that business."
Another letter I have just received from Mrs. Floyd
Souders, Cheney, Kansas, says:
"On Sunday, May 7, Dr. Charles P. Culver, Superintendent ofythe
Christian Herald Industrial Mission, Foochow, China, spoke at the
morning worship of our local Methodist church. His address reve ed
to me the dreadful effects of the Japanese war of invasion in China.
F

=== Page 417 === (confidence 0.96)
Mnited States Benate
COMMITTEE ON
AGRICULTURE AND FORESTRY
on.CH
2-
C. A. LAWTON, CLERX
Horetofore I had not realized that the United States has been
previous to February 2, selling war materials to Japan, thus
enabling her to continue this terrible war. In his talk, Dr.
Culver said that Japan could not carry on the war for three
months without the materials purchased from the United States and
two other great world powers. As I understand it, now the United
States does not send 'arms, ammunitions and implements of war' to
Japan, but we still do carry on trade with them in such raw materials
as they need to make their own bombs and other war equipment. For
example, we sell them tons of scrap iron, gasoline, oil, etc. He
urged us to not buy Japanese made articles and especially to stop
purchasing silk stockings."
I will be glad to know whether the statements that
the Unites States government is aiding Japan are justified by the facts.
Arthm apper
Sincerely yours,

=== Page 422 === (confidence 0.99)
(CORRECT COPY CO:HJG)
Mulvane, Kansas
May 13, 1939
Senator Arthur Capper
U. S. Senate
Washington, D.C.
Dear Mr. Capper:
For several days I have wanted to write you concerning
our trade with Japan. May I urge your wholehearted support
to all legislation which will stop the war sustaining traffic
with Japan. I think this is a very short-sighted measure
because if we sell munitions and other supplies to Japan, it
will help her all the more to be successful in gaining control
in China. Furthermore, if Japan does get in a position of
control of the Chinese ports, she will turn around and block
out our trade with China. I think China has great possibili-
ties for expansion but Japan will reap the benefit and the
United States will not get any if Japan is successful.
Thus as a fellow citizen of Kansas and a much interested
citizen of this country may I again urge you to wholeheartedly
support any legislation against the war sustaining racket
with respect to Japan.
Also I wish to commend you on your position on the
Ludlow Amendment. Personally, I feel that is a genuine move
toward peace and therefore I hope you hold firm and support
it to the limit.
Yours very truly,
Robert J. Blackburn

=== Page 424 === (confidence 0.99)
SENATE MEMORIAL NO. 3.
A Joint Resolution as a Memorial to Congress to pass
an Act Terminating the Selling of Munitions and War Materials
by Citizens of the United States to the Country of Japan.
WHEREAS in the interests of humanity and world peace
it becomes necessary that the President of the United States
and the Congress of the United States take such action as
will deny aid or assistance of any kind to the country of
Japan in the furtherance of the war against the country of
China; and
VHFPFAS denial of the right to purchase munitions and
materials of war would effectively accomplish such purpose;
nov, therefore
BE IT FEEOLVED BY THE LEGISLATURE OF THF STATE OF FLORIDA:
Section 1. That this body memorialize the President of
the United States and the Congress of the United States to
take immediate action to terminate the selling of munitions and
war materials by citizens of the United States to the country
of Japan.
Section 2. That the Senators and Representatives of the
State of Florida, in the Congress of the United States, give
their support to any measure that will accomplish the purpose
of this hesolution, and that copies of this Memorial be for-
warded to the President of the United States, the President of
the Senate, and the Speaker of the House of Representatives of
the Congress of the United States, and to the Senators and
Representatives of the State of Florida in Congress.
Approved by the Governor May 10, 1939.
Filed in Office Secretary of State May 1l, 1939.
